Please be advised that the author and publisher take no responsibility whatsoever
for any (and all) accidental and inadvertent self-inflicted injuries that may occur from
exploring the material in this book. (This is especially relevant in regard to the left hand
fingers, wrist, forearm, shoulder, muscles, tendons, bones, cartilage, nerves, etc., et al.)

Any guitarist who works with this book is going to be exposed to literally hun-
dreds of chords that involve stretches and fingering configurations that will be totally
new and unfamiliar. Be particularly cautious with descending voice-leadings that move
down the fingerboard. With each new descending chord, the distance between the frets
gradually increases.

Each (and every) guitarist must continuously monitor the sensations they perceive
in their left (fingering) hand. Error on the side of being overly careful. “No pain—is the
way to go.” Perhaps it would be even better to put it this way: “Not even the slightest hint
of any discomfort—is the way to go.”

The problem lies with the fact that a large percentage of this material is going to be
new to pretty much every guitarist who begins to work with it. When guitarists get a
chance to play a whole bunch of stuff that is not only new to them, but also has a uniquely
different and compelling sound to it, there is a tendency to want to overdo it. All these
new sounds can have an almost narcotic-like effect. The ear perks up; the mind comes into
sharp focus; the emotions begin to soar; the imagination begins to project a future when
you'll be able to use all this great stuff to completely blow people away(!)...all at the
expense of your left hand which, at this point, starts to feel like it’s just been run over by a
truck.

You just got caught up in “seduction by novelty.” You stopped monitoring the sen-
sations in your left (fingering) hand. You did too much. You over-extended your physical
limits. You over-stretched. You had a nice ride, for sure. But now you'll have to pay the
price for days, weeks, possibly months. (Believe me, I’m not exaggerating!) In some cases,
the damage could involve years of recovery time. Occasionally, it could cause damage
beyond recovery time. The word for that is “permanent.” The phrase in sports terminol-
ogy is “career-ending.”

Think about it —Picture yourself with permanent damage—Now what?

Q: — Mr. Goodchord, are you trying to scare us?
A: You got that right!

You must exercise your sensitivity, your intelligence, your caution and your good
judgement. No one else can do this for you. Only you can. And when you do, there should
be no problem.

You have been WARNED! (Please take good care of your hands!) Some sugges-
tions and tentative guidelines/ restrictions will follow a bit later.
“WHAT I¢ THIS BOOK?”

This is a guitar sourcebook. It was written with an eye to the future of the next
several generations of guitarists. It aims at anticipating certain “needs” shared by all gui-
tarists; those who are now old, middle-aged, young and those who are as yet unborn. It
consists of material derived from principles that are surprisingly simple and straightfor-
ward, but that imply potentials that are completely mind-blowing.

The scope and range of the material in this book (and several more that will follow,
soon enough), could easily overwhelm any single guitarist who begins to work with it.
That’s totally O.K. Be overwhelmed! (It’s good for you!) Just to give you an idea of how
much material there is, (in Volumes I & II combined) I'll relate this incident:

I recently went to Anaheim, CA to do a presentation at the 1.A,J.E. (International
Association of Jazz Educators) for Berklee College of Music (where I currently teach) re-
garding this material. I had prepared a home-recorded CD for demonstration purposes.
In order to save myself time with the recording, I did it as a guitar quartet as opposed to
playing all the 4-part chords at one time by myself. The CD contained 30 examples in C
Major, 30 examples in C Melodic Minor, and 30 examples in C Harmonic Minor. They
included all five chord-families in all 6 cycles through what I call the “short form,” (7
different chords resolving to the next inversion of the first chord) but using only 1 of 6
possible voicing types. The 90 examples totaled about 18 minutes. It represented about
4.8% of the whole thing! (Without even considering the triads and the 3-part 4ths!) We’re
talking HUGE here!

There is no need for any single guitarist to learn all this. Frankly, I think that it’s not
only impossible but, more importantly, unnecessary. (Hopefully, all of this will become
obvious after working with the material for 6 months to 1 year.)

This is a book about voice-leading. It’s a book about chords. It’s a book about using
simple mathematical ways of coming up with all the possibilities within certain pre-de-
termined and limited (although still enormous) parameters. (That's correct... ALL!)

It’s a book about a number of other things as well. Some of them are difficult (if not
impossible) to put into words...Some others are easily described:

* Better knowledge of the fingerboard

¢ Better knowledge of Major, Melodic Minor, and Harmonic Minor scales
(both melodically and harmonically)

© Better knowledge of counterpoint

¢ Better knowledge of using open strings with other, fretted notes

« Increased ability to move 2, 3, 4 (and possibly 5) voices more or less independently

¢ Increased comping skills

* Increased compositional skills

¢ Increased improvisational skills

* Increased teaching skills

© Increased humility

This is also the ultimate do-it-yourself book. How so? READ ON!

“IT WOULD SEEM THAT SOME THINGS ARE MISSING...”

Correct. Much is missing. In fact, almost everything that one would deem important is
missing! Let me list a few: .

Thines # issing in this book:

¢ Detailed instructions on what to do e Dynamics
¢ Fingerings * Articulations
* Position markers ¢ Time signatures
* Alternate fingerings ¢ Rhythm
* Open string indicators ® String sets
¢ Tempos ¢ Tablature
* Written musical notation
¢ This type of guitar info: 3
(in spite of that, check out this chord.

It’s called “the despair chord!”
<P 7
It lives in the Harmonic Major Scale...
Sorry, I couldn't resist!)

What is not in this book is as deliberately omitted as what is included. A few examples:

* Written musical notation is not used because not all guitarists can read

* Tablature is not used for the same reason, plus the fact that it’s “too fingering specific”
for our purposes

* Consequently, any guitarist (and all guitarists) who at least know where the notes
are on the instrament can use this book ;

* Pretty much all of the other “omissions” occur so that you, the individual guitarist,
have the unimpeded, uninfluenced, unprecedented and (probably) unwanted
opportunity to figure it all out by yourself! (Thus, the ultimate do-it-yourself book.)

The less that is explained, the more you have to do. (Get to do!) What you learn,
and what that will lead to, is your own business, your own discovery, your own musical
vision, your own musical direction, your own musical individuality. Ten different guitar-
ists who work with this material for at least one and hopefully two years, will come up
with ten totally different musical realities! (I’ve already seen it happen, in that a very
small number of my students over the last 4 or 5 years have been working with “just a bit”
of all this material for a while. Each and every one of them has come up with something
different!)

The main thing for me, is the “discovery” part of it. I was really lucky. I discovered
it by myself. (In retrospect, given all my musical education, great teachers, and great mu-
sical influences, I had a tremendous amount of help and preparative skill-training!) I don’t
want to take away those experiences from any one guitarist (or all guitarists) by trying to
explain too much.
“NAME THAT CHORD”

Volume I consists of triads, 7* chords, triad over bass note I and triad over bass
note II (TBN I, TBN I). Triads are 3-part chords. The other 3 voicings are 4-part chords.
Triads have simple, standardized names. No problem. 7* chords also have fairly simple
and standardized names. Also, not a problem. However, TBN J and TBN II chords have
more complex and much less standardized names. The problem involves the fact that
the name is only accurate 25% of the time, (when the bass note is actually the lowest
note of the voicing.) The other 75% of the time (the other 3 inversions), the so-called
bass-note is in the soprano (top), alto (2 from the top), or tenor (3" from the top) voice.
Consequently, the names for TBN I and TBN II structures are less than completely
satisfactory. (One could certainly make a case for the argument that, for example, Am/D
could just as easily have been called Am add D or Am add 4.) The chords are what they
are regardless of what they’re called. As long as our approach to the naming process is
consistent (and I believe it is), I think we'll be O.K. We'll just have to proceed as best we
can. (Besides, the chords couldn’t care less what we call them!)

Q: What is the difference between TBN I and TBN II?

A: You could think of it this way: Even the 7* chords could be thought of (or named) as
some kind of triad over bass note. For example, Cmaj7 could be viewed as Em/C or
even C/B in terms of arriving at the 4 different notes in the structure. However, as was
mentioned earlier, since the 7* chords terminology is more consistent and standardized,
we'll go with that. We could think of TBN I and TBN II as being built up in thirds from
the 7* chord. For example, where Em/C is Cmaj7; moving the triad (Em) up a diatonic
third produces G/C, or TBN I. Moving the triad up a third again produces B°/C, or
TBN I. Another way to think of it: In TBN I, the relationship of the triad to the bass note
is a diatonic 5"; in TBN IL, , the relationship of the triad to the bass note is a diatonic 7*.
I hope that clarifies it for you.

Q: What are cycles? Why use them?

A: Cycles are ways of moving through a scale. Cycles can be melodic or harmonic. We’re
dealing with the harmonic aspect. Since we're exploring 3 different 7-note scales, (Major,
Melodic Minor, Harmonic Minor) it turns out that there are 6 diatonic cycles. For pur-
poses of consistency, I’ve named all 6 cycles by ascending diatonic intervals.

° Cycle 2—ascending a diatonic 24, (i.e, CM7, Dm/7, Em/7, etc.)
* Cycle 4—ascending a diatonic 4“, (i.e, CM7, FM7, Bm7(b5), etc.)
* Cycle 6—ascending a diatonic 6", (Le., CM7, Am7, FM/7, etc.)
or descending a diatonic 3", (easier)
* Cycle 7—ascending a diatonic 7*, (i.e, CM7, Bm7(b5), Am/7, etc.)
or descending a diatonic 2", (easier)
¢ Cycle 5—ascending a diatonic 5*, (i.e, CM7, G7, Dm/7, etc.)
or descending a diatonic 4", (easier)
Cycle 3—ascending a diatonic 3%, (i.e, CM7, Em7, G7, etc.)

Cycles 2 and 7 are the same in opposite directions
Cycles 4 and 5 are the same in opposite directions
Cycles 6 and 3 are the same in opposite directions

*Note that what most of the musical world calls cycle 5, we will be calling cycle 4. (Sorry about that!)

The reason we use the cycles is because they cover all diatonic possibilities. There
are no other ways of moving diatonically (within a 7-note scale) that are not included in
the 6 cycles using the same harmonic structure.

All of this may seem confusing just in the reading of it. But, I’m sure that once you
begin to work with the material, it will gradually become very clear. (You could think of it
this way: using the same voicing type, if you voice-lead through all 6 cycles, you will have
moved from every chord to every other chord.)

Q: Why do cycles 2 and 7 look so different from what you'd expect to see?

A: That's a very good question. Technically speaking, we are featuring disjunct voice-
leading with the triads for cycles 2 and 7. The most obvious way would have been:

Triads: C major C Dm Em E G Am Be

Q: Why do you include 4-way close voicings?

A: Because some of them are playable as chords. It’s also interesting to use open strings
with other fretted notes. But the main reason they’re included is because all of them are
playable as arpeggios. This is also true with some other seemingly impossible-to-play
voicings. (Some of the Drop 2 & 3, for example.) If you can’t play a voicing as a chord, you
can always play the notes in some kind of arpeggio.

Q: Why Major scale, Melodic Minor scale, Harmonic Minor scale?

A: If music was painting, the Major, Melodic Minor, Harmonic Minor scales would be the
equivalent of the colors red, yellow and blue. These three 7-note scales have been around
for thousands of years. You'll come to see that they contain incredible harmonic variety
and richness.

Q: Are there any other ways of voice-leading through the cycles?

A: Absolutely! What we're doing in Vol. I and II is just simple common-tone voice-lead-
ing. Other ways of voice-leading will be discussed in a future volume.

Q: What is M.S.R.P.?

A:M.S.R.P. stands for Melodic Strand Replication Procedure. It’s aname I came up with to
describe some very interesting things that showed up while I was figuring out all these
voice-leadings. Again, I’m deliberately not going to go into much detail about all of this so
that each person can “re-discover” it just like I did.

On each page you'll see Intervallic Voice-leading, Functional Voice-leading, (of which
there are two different depictions) and M.S.R_P. at the bottom. All of them are essentially
the same thing. Just different ways of looking at it. However, it does get really interesting
when you see that all of these chords and voicings are also melodies. (Actually, in most
cases, a single melody!) So this is the musical version of “What came first; the chicken or
the egg?” i.e, “What came first; melody or harmony?” (to which I would reply to both
questions: “Both arose simultaneously.”) M.S.R.P. is like melodic D.N.A. that makes up
harmony. Good luck!!

Q: You mentioned earlier that you would be giving tentative guidelines concerning the left
(fingering) hand. What are they?

A: Ilied....sort of!

Actually, that’s very difficult to do, given all the possible combinations of different hand
and finger sizes; long, medium and short scale necks; high, medium and low string
tensions; high, medium and low string actions, etc., etc., etc.

However, the main thing is to follow the advice given in the DIS@LAIMER/WARNING/
CAUTIONI®! Section. (Go back and read it again, right now!) If everyone does that we'll
all be just fine!
In addition, here are some other points to consider:
° Be cautious of any stretches greater than 4 frets beneath the V* position.
¢ Remember that arpeggiating the notes is always an option.
* You can, at any time, transpose the voicing up one octave, thus making the
stretch considerably less.

Keep in mind that voicings which seem “too much of a stretch” look very
different in other keys.

Q: Isn’t it true that musicians who play other harmonic instruments besides guitar could learn a
lot by exploring this material?

A: Yes. But please don’t tell them!

Q: Wouldn't composers and arrangers benefit as well, especially considering that they don’t have to
deal with the limitations of the guitar?

A: Shhhhi!!!

Q: When can I start to move “all this stuff” to other keys? And what key should I choose?

A: Same answer to both questions: You don’t move it. Itmoves by itself. Just watch! (Don’t
be in a hurry——) When it does move, don’t assume that you shouldn’t continue with “all
this stuff” in C Major, C Melodic Minor and C Harmonic Minor. (How much of this mate-
rial do you really think you have explored, let alone learned?)

Q: What is the best way to learn all this stuff?

A: There is no best way

Q: O.K. Ah....Is there a worst way?

A: Yes! By trying to find the best way.

Q: O.K. Al... ceecccaes Do you have any suggestions?

A: Yes. Don’t try to learn it. In fact, try not to learn it!

Q: I don’t think I understand that.

A: If you don’t try to learn it, if you try not to learn it, you completely eliminate any self-
imposed (or “other-imposed”) pressure. You just have the experiences of going through

the material. That will be enough.

10
There are so many aspects and ways in which a guitarist can learn “all this stuff,”
that you don’t want to short-change yourself by “learning” it too fast. Just consider some
things about how a guitarist learns:

© Tactile (touch)

Shape (configuration-visual)
Movement (kinesthetic)
Repetition (memory)

¢ Simultaneous melodies (gulp!)
© Understanding (analysis-theoretical)
¢ Sonic (sound)

¢ Emotional (feel)

© Gestalt (context-function)

© Other

¢ Unknown

* Etc., etc., etc.

It’s certainly possible that a guitarist could learn some of this material very quickly.
{I've already seen this happen with a few of my students.) The obvious plus-side is that
they can repeat a cycle with a voicing through a scale from memory. And that’s O.K. But
the less obvious minus-side is that they can’t really use any of that material in a musical
context. They didn’t give themselves the chance to “learn all this stuff” in many different
ways at many different levels.

Don’t be in a hurry. Take your time. Find your own pace. Relax into your own
learning process, and let the material do to you (and with you) what it (and you) need. It
and you both need time to grow.

I realize that what follows may sound a little weird, or a bit of a stretch (pun in-
tended), but I'll say it anyway have faith that the structure, consistency, scope and
approach to this material (“all this stuff”) has an intelligence all it’s own. It’s almost as if it
were alive. If you work with it, it will “speak” to you. It will tell you things. It will present
you with possibilities that you had NEVER considered before. It will give you incredible
“bits and pieces” that you will feel compelled to extend and complete.

Let me leave it at that. Check it out!

—Mick Gaadrick Basten, July 2000
